"
The best methods to scrape or handle a website depend on the site's complexity, your coding comfort level, and the scale of the project. [1]
1. By Site Complexity and Technology

* Static HTML Websites (No JavaScript):
   * Best Tools: Python with `requests` and [Beautiful Soup](https://www.upgrad.com/blog/top-final-year-computer-science-projects-topics/).
   * How it works: Pulls the raw HTML directly from the server and parses the tag tree to extract text, links, or tables. It is fast, lightweight, and ideal for blogs or basic directories. [1, 2]
* Dynamic & JavaScript-Heavy Websites (React, Vue, Infinite Scroll):
   * Best Tools: Headless browsers like Playwright, Puppeteer, or Selenium.
   * How it works: Spins up a real (headless) browser engine to execute JavaScript, wait for DOM assembly, and simulate user interactions like scrolling or clicking. [1, 3, 4]
* Large-Scale Crawling & Data Pipelines:
   * Best Tools: [Scrapy](https://scrapy.org/).
   * How it works: A full-fledged Python framework designed for high-speed, asynchronous, multi-page crawling and structured data export.

2. No-Code and AI-Driven Approaches

* Visual Point-and-Click Scrapers:
   * Best Tools: Octoparse or [Browse AI](https://zapier.com/blog/web-scraping/).
   * How it works: You click through a visual browser interface to "train" a robot on what fields or lists to grab, handling pagination and dynamic content automatically. [3, 5]
* AI & Managed Scraping APIs:
   * Best Tools: Firecrawl, ScrapingBee, or Diffbot.
   * How it works: These tools convert unstructured HTML directly into clean markdown or structured JSON using AI schemas, while automatically handling proxy rotation, CAPTCHAs, and anti-bot defenses. [2, 4, 6, 7]

Best Practices for Handling Websites Responsibly

* Check `robots.txt` and Terms of Service: Review the website’s `/robots.txt` file to see which paths are restricted from crawlers.
* Set Rate Limits and Delays: Add a 1 to 3-second delay between requests or limit concurrency so you do not overload the host server.
* Identify Your Bot: Include a custom `User-Agent` header in your requests with a project URL or contact email so site owners know who you are.
* Use Official APIs First: If the website offers a public or commercial JSON/REST API, use it instead of scraping HTML.
* Rotate Proxies: For high-volume or heavily protected sites, use residential or data center proxy pools to prevent IP bans. [1, 4, 8]

If you tell me what specific website or data you are trying to collect, and your programming experience (code vs. no-code), I can recommend the exact tool and setup for your project.

[1] https://www.youtube.com
[2] https://www.firecrawl.dev
[3] https://medium.com
[4] https://www.scrapingbee.com
[5] https://zapier.com
[6] https://www.youtube.com
[7] https://browser-use.com
[8] https://www.screenshotengine.com"

<pasted_content id="614c">
You are Claude working in plan mode on the QuoteCraft RFQ-to-bid agent
for the Atira EHL challenge.

Project context
- The agent runs against 4 websites (tender portal, supplier portal, ERP,
  mail) and currently reaches quality 100 and full eligibility on the
  public dataset.
- The best scored run (run_a176359f, restored in commit 95f075b) has
  time ≈ 00:06:15 and cost ≈ $0.176 on the public leaderboard.
- BEST_RUN_TIMING.md and SPEED_PLAN.md already analyze that run and list
  the main bottlenecks.

Primary objective
- Reduce runtime by at least 60 seconds (target ≈ 4:45–5:15) on the
  public test environment, WITHOUT losing eligibility or reducing
  quality below 100.
- Do this by tightening agent orchestration and browser timings, not by
  changing the RFQ/bid logic or pricing math.

Hard constraints (do NOT violate)
1. Keep the agent fully eligible: every required field must remain
   correct and complete, and the bid must still be submitted.
2. Preserve quality 100: no degradation in field-level correctness or
   evidence handling is acceptable.
3. Do not change:
   - discount and price calculation code,
   - RFQ interpretation logic,
   - bid-to-form reconciliation and pre-submit checks.
4. Keep model cost in the same order of magnitude; do not triple token
   usage just to gain speed.
5. Make changes in small, reviewable steps: one clearly scoped “fix”
   per test run, with comments in SPEED_PLAN.md.

Known time sinks (from BEST_RUN_TIMING.md)
A. Tender portal login hang (~128 s wasted)
   - A click falls back to Playwright’s long default timeout, then a
     long page-read limit; the tab is only replaced after repeated
     failures.
B. Tender portal extra browsing (~41 s wasted)
   - After collecting the needed RFQ facts, the agent keeps reopening
     documents and waiting for “back” operations that don’t add new
     information.
C. Position entry in the bid form (~110 s total)
   - The clerk agent enters positions one dropdown choice per turn,
     with one model call and one browser action per tiny step, leading
     to ≈22 s per position.

Your tasks

Task 1 – Login hang fix (Fix #1)
- Refactor the tender portal agent’s click/wait helper so that:
  - Clicks to login and post-login navigation use a SHORT timeout
    (e.g. 8–10 s) and at most 1–2 retries.
  - Success detection is based on specific DOM markers (e.g. presence
    of the tender dashboard or RFQ list), not on waiting an arbitrary
    30–45 s.
  - Page reads on the tender portal use a tighter cap (e.g. 10–15 s)
    and return as soon as the accessibility tree is stable.
- Ensure failure handling:
  - On a timeout, refresh or reopen the tab once, then retry;
  - If login still fails, log a clear error but do not sit idle for
    another 45 s.
- Update or add tests for the login helper so we can assert:
  - fast success path,
  - controlled failure path with bounded wait,
  - no infinite or 60+ second hangs.

Task 2 – Stop tender agent when its job is done (Fix #2)
- Introduce an explicit “tender_evidence_complete” state:
  - When RFQ specs, positions, Q&A, and any required documents are
    successfully extracted and filed, mark the tender agent as DONE.
- Change the tender agent’s planning logic so that once DONE is reached:
  - It stops proposing navigation actions on the tender portal,
  - It does not reopen already-filed documents,
  - It yields control back to the coordinator.
- Add tests to ensure:
  - After required tender data is present in the shared state, no more
    redundant “open document / back / wait” cycles occur.
  - The agent still extracts all needed tender evidence before stopping.

Task 3 – Batch position entry (Fix #3)
- Redesign the clerk agent’s form-filling strategy so that each turn
  fills an entire position or a large chunk of it, instead of one
  dropdown per turn.
  Concretely:
  - For each position, compose a structured action that sets all fields
    that can be filled from the current bid state in ONE browser step
    (or in at most two steps: one to open the row, one to apply all
    field values).
  - Reduce the number of model turns per position to 1–2, targeting
    ≈5–8 s per position instead of ≈22 s.
- Keep safety:
  - Continue to rely on deterministic code for calculations,
    indices, and value selection.
  - Keep the pre-submit checklist to verify the live form matches the
    intended bid after the batched fill.
- Add tests that:
  - Populate a mock form with multiple positions;
  - Verify that one agent turn correctly fills all fields for a
    position;
  - Confirm that the reconciliation gate still catches mismatches.

Task 4 – Measurement and documentation
- After each fix is implemented:
  - Run a sandbox test on the same environment as run_a176359f.
  - Record time and cost in BEST_RUN_TIMING.md and SPEED_PLAN.md.
  - Explain briefly which fix was applied and how it changed timing
    (e.g. “login hang reduced from 128 s wasted to <20 s total”).
- Only stack fixes together after each one has been validated alone:
  - First run: Fix #1 only, on top of the restored best package.
  - Second run: Fix #1 + Fix #2.
  - Third run: Fix #1 + Fix #2 + Fix #3.
  - Stop stacking if any fix causes a regression in eligibility or
    quality.

Workstyle requirements
- Use plan mode: first list the files you will edit and the diffs you
  propose, then apply them.
- Preserve the structure of the existing multi-agent design; do not
  introduce new agents or major refactors.
- Keep code changes tight and well-commented around the timing-critical
  paths (login helper, tender agent planner, clerk form-filling).
- When you are done, summarize:
  - The new measured runtime and cost,
  - Which bottlenecks were removed,
  - Which risks were introduced and how they are mitigated.

Goal check
- The work is successful only if:
  - The agent still produces an eligible, quality-100 bid, and
  - Runtime is reduced by at least 60 seconds compared to 6:15, with
    no major cost blow-up.
